0.002.00
−0.251.00
−0.450.20
−0.500.00
−0.650.00

จุดสุดท้ายที่ยังมีกระแสและจุดแรกที่อ่านได้ศูนย์บอกช่วงใด? แยกค่า V ที่มีเครื่องหมายออกจากขนาด Vₛ

ที่ −0.45 V ยังอ่านได้ 0.20 µA ที่ −0.50 V อ่านได้ศูนย์ ข้อมูลสองจุดจึงให้ 0.45 < Vₛ ≤ 0.50 V ค่าประมาณจากจุดแรกที่เป็นศูนย์คือ V = −0.50 V หรือ Vₛ ≈ 0.50 V ชุดสอนตั้งให้จุดหยุดจริงในแบบจำลองอยู่ตรงค่านี้

ทำนายแล้วลอง · ในโหมด «สำรวจหลอดเอง» ใช้โลหะ A แสงชุดสอน ความเข้ม 50% และ V = −0.55 V ถ้าเพิ่มความถี่เป็น 8.00 × 10¹⁴ Hz จะเริ่มมีกระแสไหม? บันทึกค่าก่อนและหลังเพื่อเช็ก

PHYSICS · 19.2 · ปรากฏการณ์โฟโตอิเล็กทริกRevision Notes · อ่านทบทวน

จากผลวัดสู่กราฟ อัตราเร็ว และจำนวนโฟตอน

ให้ Vₛ เป็นขนาดศักย์หยุดยั้งและ V เป็นศักย์ขั้วรับเทียบขั้วปล่อย เมื่อกวาดแรงดันไปทางลบ ใช้ Vₛ ≈ −V ที่จุดแรกที่อ่านกระแสเป็นศูนย์

แทนพลังงานจลน์จากสมการ (2) ในสมการ (4) แล้วหารทั้งสมการด้วยขนาดประจุอิเล็กตรอน e

\(eV_s=hf-W\)

\(V_s=\frac{h}{e}f-\frac{W}{e}\)(5)

บนกราฟ Vₛ กับ f ความชันคือ h/e จุดตัดแกนนอนคือ f₀ และจุดตัดแกนตั้งจากแนวเส้นต่อคือ −W/e

ให้ mₑ เป็นมวลอิเล็กตรอนหน่วย kg และ vₘₐₓ เป็นอัตราเร็วสูงสุดหน่วย m/s ใช้นิยามพลังงานจลน์แบบไม่สัมพัทธภาพ โดยคิดพลังงานหน่วย J

\(K_{\max}=\frac{1}{2}m_ev_{\max}^2\)(6)

ให้ I ของแสงเป็นความเข้มแสงหน่วย W/m², A เป็นพื้นที่รับแสงหน่วย m² และ Δt เป็นช่วงเวลาหน่วย s สำหรับแสงตกตั้งฉาก ความเข้มคงที่คือพลังงานรวมต่อพื้นที่ต่อเวลา

\(I_{\mathrm{light}}=\frac{E_{\mathrm{total}}}{A\Delta t}\)

\(E_{\mathrm{total}}=I_{\mathrm{light}}A\Delta t\)(7)

ถ้าโฟตอนทุกตัวมีพลังงาน Eγ เท่ากัน ให้ N เป็นจำนวนโฟตอนที่ตกกระทบ พลังงานรวมคือผลรวมของโฟตอนทั้ง N ตัว

\(E_{\mathrm{total}}=NE_\gamma\)

\(N=\frac{E_{\mathrm{total}}}{E_\gamma}\)(8)

PHYSICS · 19.2 · ปรากฏการณ์โฟโตอิเล็กทริกRevision Notes · อ่านทบทวน

ตัวอย่าง 1–2 · คำนวณจากพลังงานโฟตอน

ค่าที่ใช้คำนวณ · \(h=6.626\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s}\) · \(c=3.00\times10^8\,\mathrm{m/s}\) · \(1\,\mathrm{eV}=1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\) · \(1\,\mathrm{nm}=10^{-9}\,\mathrm m\)

ตัวอย่าง 1 · พลังงานของโฟตอน

แสงในสุญญากาศมีความยาวคลื่น 413.3 nm จงคำนวณพลังงานของโฟตอนหนึ่งตัวในหน่วยจูลและอิเล็กตรอนโวลต์

ใช้สมการ (1)

\[\begin{aligned}\lambda&=413.3\times10^{-9}\,\mathrm m \\ E_\gamma&=\frac{hc}{\lambda}=\frac{(6.626\times10^{-34})(3.00\times10^8)}{413.3\times10^{-9}}=4.81\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J \\ E_\gamma&=\frac{4.81\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J}{1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm{J/eV}}\approx3.00\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

โฟตอนหนึ่งตัวมีพลังงานประมาณ 3.00 eV

ตัวอย่าง 2 · หาฟังก์ชันงานจากความถี่ขีดเริ่ม

โลหะ A มีความถี่ขีดเริ่มประมาณ 6.04 × 10¹⁴ Hz จงคำนวณฟังก์ชันงานใน eV และพลังงานจลน์สูงสุดเมื่อฉายโฟตอน 3.00 eV

ใช้สมการ (3), (2)

\[\begin{aligned}W&=hf_0=(6.626\times10^{-34})(6.04\times10^{14})=4.00\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\approx2.50\,\mathrm{eV} \\ K_{\max}&=E_\gamma-W=(3.00-2.50)\,\mathrm{eV}=0.50\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

โฟตอนมีพลังงานมากกว่า W จึงเกิดการหลุด

PHYSICS · 19.2 · ปรากฏการณ์โฟโตอิเล็กทริกRevision Notes · อ่านทบทวน

ตัวอย่าง 3–4 · ตรวจการหลุดและเทียบโลหะ

ค่าที่ใช้คำนวณ · \(h=6.626\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s}\) · \(c=3.00\times10^8\,\mathrm{m/s}\) · \(1\,\mathrm{eV}=1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\) · \(1\,\mathrm{nm}=10^{-9}\,\mathrm m\)

ตัวอย่าง 3 · ความยาวคลื่นที่ยาวเกินขีดเริ่ม

ฉายแสงความยาวคลื่น 600 nm ลงบนโลหะ A ที่มีฟังก์ชันงาน 2.50 eV จงตัดสินว่าอิเล็กตรอนหลุดหรือไม่ ถ้าไม่หลุด พลังงานต่อโฟตอนขาดอีกเท่าไรจึงถึงขีดเริ่ม?

ใช้สมการ (1)

\[\begin{aligned}E_\gamma&=\frac{hc}{\lambda}=\frac{(6.626\times10^{-34})(3.00\times10^8)}{600\times10^{-9}}=3.31\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\approx2.07\,\mathrm{eV} \\ W-E_\gamma&\approx2.50-2.07=0.43\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

ไม่มีการปล่อยอิเล็กตรอน ต้องเพิ่มพลังงานต่อโฟตอนประมาณ 0.43 eV จึงถึงขีดเริ่ม

ตัวอย่าง 4 · ฉายแสงชุดเดียวกันลงบนโลหะสามชนิด

ฉายโฟตอนพลังงาน 3.00 eV ลงบนโลหะ A, B และ C ซึ่งมีฟังก์ชันงาน 2.50, 2.80 และ 3.10 eV ตามลำดับ โลหะใดปล่อยอิเล็กตรอน และอิเล็กตรอนจากแต่ละโลหะที่ปล่อยได้มีพลังงานจลน์สูงสุดกี่ eV?

ใช้สมการ (2)

\[\begin{aligned}K_{\max,\mathrm A}&=E_\gamma-W_{\mathrm A}=(3.00-2.50)\,\mathrm{eV}=0.50\,\mathrm{eV} \\ K_{\max,\mathrm B}&=E_\gamma-W_{\mathrm B}=(3.00-2.80)\,\mathrm{eV}=0.20\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

A และ B ปล่อยโฟโตอิเล็กตรอน ส่วน C มี W สูงกว่า 3.00 eV จึงไม่เกิดการหลุด

PHYSICS · 19.2 · ปรากฏการณ์โฟโตอิเล็กทริกRevision Notes · อ่านทบทวน

ตัวอย่าง 5–6 · ใช้กราฟหาค่าที่ต้องการ

ตัวอย่าง 5 · หาค่าคงที่ของพลังค์จากจุดบนกราฟ

ฉายแสงลงบนโลหะ A ที่ 7.00 × 10¹⁴ Hz วัดพลังงานจลน์สูงสุดได้ 0.395 eV และที่ 9.00 × 10¹⁴ Hz วัดได้ 1.222 eV จงคำนวณความชันของกราฟพลังงานจลน์สูงสุดกับความถี่ใน eV·s และใช้ข้อมูลจุดแรกหาฟังก์ชันงานใน eV

ใช้สมการ (2)

\[\begin{aligned}h&=\frac{\Delta K_{\max}}{\Delta f}=\frac{1.222-0.395}{(9.00-7.00)\times10^{14}}=4.14\times10^{-15}\,\mathrm{eV\,s} \\ W&=hf-K_{\max}=(4.135\times10^{-15})(7.00\times10^{14})-0.395\approx2.50\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

เก็บความชันก่อนปัดไว้ใช้คำนวณ W

ตัวอย่าง 6 · อ่านกราฟชุดสอนแล้วย้อนหาฟังก์ชันงาน

ใช้โฟตอนพลังงาน 3.00 eV กราฟกระแสของชุดสอนอ่านได้ 0.20 µA ที่ศักย์ขั้วรับ −0.45 V และเริ่มอ่านได้ 0.00 µA ที่ −0.50 V จงประมาณขนาดศักย์หยุดยั้งใน V แล้วหาพลังงานจลน์สูงสุดและฟังก์ชันงานใน eV

ใช้สมการ (4), (2)

\[\begin{aligned}V&\approx-0.50\,\mathrm V,\qquad V_s\approx0.50\,\mathrm V \\ K_{\max}&=eV_s\approx(1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm C)(0.50\,\mathrm V)=8.01\times10^{-20}\,\mathrm J=0.50\,\mathrm{eV} \\ W&=E_\gamma-K_{\max}\approx3.00-0.50=2.50\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

ข้อมูลที่วัดคร่อม 0.45 < Vₛ ≤ 0.50 V การคำนวณใช้จุดศูนย์แรกเป็นค่าประมาณ

ตัวอย่าง 7 · จากผลวัดสู่กราฟศักย์หยุดยั้ง

กลับไปที่ซิม เลือก «สำรวจหลอดเอง» แล้วใช้ชุดวัด 3 ความถี่ของโลหะ A ที่ความเข้ม 50% จดค่าแรงดันสุดท้ายที่ยังมีกระแสและแรงดันแรกที่เป็นศูนย์ ซึ่งห่างกัน 0.01 V

f (10¹⁴ Hz)V สุดท้ายที่ I > 0 (V)V แรกที่ I = 0 (V)Vₛ (V)
6.50−0.18−0.190.19
7.25−0.49−0.500.50
8.00−0.80−0.810.81

ใช้สมการ (5) กับค่า Vₛ จากตาราง

พล็อตจุดบนกราฟ หาแนวเส้นตรงจากค่าที่วัด แล้วต่อเส้นประเพื่ออ่านจุดตัดแกน ค่าที่วัดจริงอยู่เหนือขีดเริ่ม

ใช้สมการ (5)

\[\begin{aligned}\frac{h}{e}&\approx\frac{0.81-0.19}{(8.00-6.50)\times10^{14}}=4.13\times10^{-15}\,\mathrm{V\,s} \\ h&=e\frac{h}{e}\approx(1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm C)(4.13\times10^{-15}\,\mathrm{V\,s})\approx6.62\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s} \\ f_0&\approx6.04\times10^{14}\,\mathrm{Hz} \\ -\frac{W}{e}&\approx-2.50\,\mathrm V\quad\Rightarrow\quad W\approx2.50\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

ถ้าเปลี่ยนโลหะ ความชันยังเท่าเดิม แต่ตำแหน่งจุดตัดเปลี่ยนตาม W

Learning Hubหน้า 11 / 13
PHYSICS · 19.2 · ปรากฏการณ์โฟโตอิเล็กทริกRevision Notes · อ่านทบทวน

ตัวอย่าง 8–9 · อัตราเร็วและจำนวนอนุภาค

ตัวอย่าง 8 · จากศักย์หยุดยั้งสู่อัตราเร็ว

จากแล็บโลหะ A มีศักย์หยุดยั้งประมาณ 0.50 V จงหาอัตราเร็วสูงสุดของอิเล็กตรอน กำหนดมวลอิเล็กตรอน 9.11 × 10⁻³¹ kg

ใช้สมการ (4), (6)

\[\begin{aligned}K_{\max}&=eV_s=(1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm C)(0.50\,\mathrm V)=8.01\times10^{-20}\,\mathrm J \\ v_{\max}&=\sqrt{\frac{2K_{\max}}{m_e}}\approx\sqrt{\frac{2(8.01\times10^{-20}\,\mathrm J)}{9.11\times10^{-31}\,\mathrm{kg}}}\approx4.19\times10^5\,\mathrm{m/s}\end{aligned}\]

ใช้พลังงานจลน์หน่วย J เพื่อหาอัตราเร็วใน m/s

ตัวอย่าง 9 · จากความเข้มแสงสู่จำนวนโฟตอน

แสงความเข้ม 0.20 W/m² ตกตั้งฉากกับพื้นที่ 2.0 cm² เป็นเวลา 5.0 s โฟตอนแต่ละตัวมีพลังงาน 4.0 × 10⁻¹⁹ J จงหาจำนวนโฟตอนที่ตกกระทบ สมมติว่าทุกโฟตอนถูกดูดกลืนและทำให้อิเล็กตรอนหลุดหนึ่งตัว จะมีอิเล็กตรอนหลุดกี่ตัว?

ใช้สมการ (7), (8)

\[\begin{aligned}E_{\mathrm{total}}&=I_{\mathrm{light}} A\Delta t=(0.20\,\mathrm{W/m^2})(2.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2})(5.0\,\mathrm s)=2.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm J \\ N&=\frac{E_{\mathrm{total}}}{E_\gamma}=\frac{2.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm J}{4.0\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J}=5.0\times10^{14}\end{aligned}\]

ได้โฟตอนและอิเล็กตรอนจำนวนเท่ากันตามสมมติฐานที่กำหนด

PHYSICS · 19.2 · ปรากฏการณ์โฟโตอิเล็กทริกRevision Notes · อ่านทบทวน

ตัวอย่าง 10 · แสงเดียวกันกับโลหะสองชนิด

ตัวอย่าง 10 · ใช้ขีดเริ่มของโลหะแรกหาฟังก์ชันงานของโลหะที่สอง

ใช้แสงที่ความถี่ขีดเริ่มของโลหะตัวอย่าง X ซึ่งมีฟังก์ชันงาน 3.60 eV ไปฉายลงบนโลหะ Y วัดศักย์หยุดยั้งได้ 0.90 V จงหาฟังก์ชันงานของ Y

ใช้สมการ (3), (4), (2)

\[\begin{aligned}E_\gamma&=hf_{0,X}=W_X=3.60\,\mathrm{eV} \\ K_{\max,Y}&=eV_s=0.90\,\mathrm{eV} \\ W_Y&=E_\gamma-K_{\max,Y}=(3.60-0.90)\,\mathrm{eV}=2.70\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

ความถี่ของแสงชุดเดิมเท่าเดิมเมื่อเปลี่ยนโลหะ

อ่านเสริม · แบบจำลองโฟโตอิเล็กทริก · คู่มือครูฟิสิกส์ สสวท. เล่ม 6

Quick Start Note · From light to the meter reading

Open the Photoelectric Effect Lab and follow the mission steps. Change the specified setting, watch the electron dots in the tube, read I (µA), and press “Record meter reading”. Transfer your readings to this page.

Compare light off and on

Light intensity (%) is relative to the highest setting in the model. The highest setting is 100%, and 50% is half that level.

Mission 1 · Use metal A and a collector voltage of +1.00 V. Light off: 0.00 µA. Teaching light at 3.00 eV and 50% intensity: 2.00 µA.

What do the readings before and after switching on the light show?

0.00 → 2.00 µA. The measured current shows that electrons travelled from the metal to the collector.

Learning HubPage 10 / 23
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Energy of a single photon

In the photon model, an electron at the metal can absorb all the energy of one photon. The delivered energy depends on the light frequency.

SymbolMeaning
\(E_\gamma\)Energy per photon, in J or eV
\(f\)Light frequency, in Hz
\(\lambda\)Wavelength in vacuum, in m
\(h\)Planck constant · \(6.626\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s}\)
\(c\)Speed of light in vacuum · \(3.00\times10^8\,\mathrm{m/s}\)

Quantum theory gives energy proportional to frequency. In vacuum, \(c=f\lambda\), so frequency is light speed divided by wavelength.

\(E_\gamma=hf=\frac{hc}{\lambda}\)(1)

\(1\,\mathrm{eV}=1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\qquad 1\,\mathrm{nm}=10^{-9}\,\mathrm m\)

At fixed frequency, how does raising intensity change the energy per photon and the number of photons incident per second?

Energy per photon is unchanged by equation (1). More photons arrive each second over the same illuminated area.

Learning HubPage 11 / 23
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Work function and the emission threshold

The work function \(W\) is the minimum energy required for an electron to escape the metal surface. It depends on the metal and surface condition. The remaining energy is kinetic. Electrons requiring the least escape energy have the maximum kinetic energy \(K_{\max}\). With consistent units, energy conservation gives \(hf=W+K_{\max}\).

\(K_{\max}=hf-W\)(2)

Let \(f_0\) be the threshold frequency. At the threshold, the photon energy just matches the work function, and equation (2) gives zero maximum kinetic energy in the ideal limit.

\(W=hf_0\)(3)
Light frequencyEmission from the metal
\(f\lt f_0\)The photon energy is insufficient. No photoelectrons are emitted.
\(f=f_0\)Ideal threshold: the energy just suffices and maximum kinetic energy is zero. The lab displays a current once frequency exceeds the threshold.
\(f>f_0\)Emission is possible and maximum kinetic energy is positive.

In the table on the first page, why does the meter still read 0.00 µA at 6.00 × 10¹⁴ Hz when intensity rises from 50% to 80%?

More photons arrive, but by equation (1) each still carries the same energy, below the work function in equation (3).

Learning HubPage 12 / 23
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Metal thresholds and the energy graph

Mission 4 · Compare your class readings with the model table for three metals at 50% intensity and +1.00 V collector voltage.

Frequency (10¹⁴ Hz)A (µA)B (µA)C (µA)
6.402.000.000.00
7.102.002.000.00
7.602.002.002.00

Why can changing the metal at the same frequency reduce the current to zero?

At 6.40, A gives a current. At 7.10, B gives a current when compared with C. At 7.60, C gives a current. By equation (3), larger W requires larger f₀.

Compare equation (2) with a straight-line equation. Frequency is on the horizontal axis and maximum kinetic energy is on the vertical axis. All slopes equal h in consistent units. Each horizontal intercept is f₀. Extending a line to f = 0 gives a vertical intercept of −W.

Graph-reading reference · 2025 A-Level Physics, question 22

Learning HubPage 13 / 23
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

From emission to the meter reading

The lab defines \(V=V_{\mathrm{collector}}-V_{\mathrm{emitter}}\). Positive V makes the collector attract electrons. With negative V, the electric field slows electrons moving toward the collector.

The electron has charge −e, where e is the elementary charge magnitude. Its electric potential energy changes by \(\Delta U=(-e)V\). When retarding voltage just brings the current to zero, let \(V_s\) be the stopping-potential magnitude. The signed axis reading is \(V=-V_s\). The fastest electrons need \(eV_s\) of energy to overcome the retarding field and reach the collection threshold.

\(K_{\max}=eV_s\)(4)

Use e = 1.602 × 10⁻¹⁹ C to answer in joules. For one electron, the numerical value of the energy in eV equals the numerical stopping-potential magnitude in volts.

The meter reads zero at 6.00 × 10¹⁴ Hz in Mission 2 and under teaching light at V = −0.50 V in Mission 3. What is different in the tube?

In Mission 2, the frequency is too low to emit electrons. In Mission 3, the light still causes emission, but the retarding voltage prevents electrons from reaching the collector.

At −0.45 V the current is 0.20 µA. At −0.50 V it is zero. The two samples bracket 0.45 < Vₛ ≤ 0.50 V. The first zero gives V ≈ −0.50 V or Vₛ ≈ 0.50 V, which equals the exact model cutoff for this preset.

PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

From readings to a graph, speed and photon count

Vₛ is the stopping-potential magnitude and V is the collector voltage relative to the emitter. When sweeping toward negative voltage, estimate Vₛ as −V at the first zero-current reading.

Substitute the kinetic energy from equation (2) into (4), then divide by the electron charge magnitude e.

\(eV_s=hf-W\)

\(V_s=\frac{h}{e}f-\frac{W}{e}\)(5)

On the Vₛ versus f graph, the slope is h/e, the horizontal intercept is f₀, and the extrapolated vertical intercept is −W/e.

Let mₑ be the electron mass in kg and vₘₐₓ the maximum speed in m/s. Use the nonrelativistic kinetic energy in joules.

\(K_{\max}=\frac{1}{2}m_ev_{\max}^2\)(6)

Let I_light be irradiance in W/m², A the illuminated area in m², and Δt the time interval in seconds. For normally incident light with constant irradiance, intensity is total energy per area per time.

\(I_{\mathrm{light}}=\frac{E_{\mathrm{total}}}{A\Delta t}\)

\(E_{\mathrm{total}}=I_{\mathrm{light}}A\Delta t\)(7)

If every photon carries the same energy Eγ, let N be the incident photon count. The total energy is the sum of the energies of all N photons.

\(E_{\mathrm{total}}=NE_\gamma\)

\(N=\frac{E_{\mathrm{total}}}{E_\gamma}\)(8)
Learning HubPage 16 / 23
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Examples 1–2 · Calculations from photon energy

Calculation data · \(h=6.626\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s}\) · \(c=3.00\times10^8\,\mathrm{m/s}\) · \(1\,\mathrm{eV}=1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\) · \(1\,\mathrm{nm}=10^{-9}\,\mathrm m\)

Example 1 · Photon energy

Light in vacuum has a wavelength of 413.3 nm. Calculate the energy of one photon in joules and electronvolts.

Use equation (1)

\[\begin{aligned}\lambda&=413.3\times10^{-9}\,\mathrm m \\ E_\gamma&=\frac{hc}{\lambda}=\frac{(6.626\times10^{-34})(3.00\times10^8)}{413.3\times10^{-9}}=4.81\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J \\ E_\gamma&=\frac{4.81\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J}{1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm{J/eV}}\approx3.00\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Each photon carries about 3.00 eV.

Example 2 · Work function from threshold frequency

Metal A has a threshold frequency of about 6.04 × 10¹⁴ Hz. Find the work function in eV and the maximum kinetic energy under 3.00 eV photons.

Use equation (3), (2)

\[\begin{aligned}W&=hf_0=(6.626\times10^{-34})(6.04\times10^{14})=4.00\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\approx2.50\,\mathrm{eV} \\ K_{\max}&=E_\gamma-W=(3.00-2.50)\,\mathrm{eV}=0.50\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Photon energy exceeds W, so emission occurs.

Learning HubPage 17 / 23
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Examples 3–4 · Check emission and compare metals

Calculation data · \(h=6.626\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s}\) · \(c=3.00\times10^8\,\mathrm{m/s}\) · \(1\,\mathrm{eV}=1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\) · \(1\,\mathrm{nm}=10^{-9}\,\mathrm m\)

Example 3 · A wavelength beyond threshold

Shine 600 nm light on metal A, whose work function is 2.50 eV. Decide whether electrons are emitted. If there is no emission, how much more energy per photon is needed to reach the threshold?

Use equation (1)

\[\begin{aligned}E_\gamma&=\frac{hc}{\lambda}=\frac{(6.626\times10^{-34})(3.00\times10^8)}{600\times10^{-9}}=3.31\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\approx2.07\,\mathrm{eV} \\ W-E_\gamma&\approx2.50-2.07=0.43\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

There is no emission. Each photon needs about 0.43 eV more to reach the threshold.

Example 4 · One light setting, three metals

Shine 3.00 eV photons on metals A, B and C, whose work functions are 2.50, 2.80 and 3.10 eV, respectively. Which metals emit electrons, and what is the maximum kinetic energy in eV for each emitting metal?

Use equation (2)

\[\begin{aligned}K_{\max,\mathrm A}&=E_\gamma-W_{\mathrm A}=(3.00-2.50)\,\mathrm{eV}=0.50\,\mathrm{eV} \\ K_{\max,\mathrm B}&=E_\gamma-W_{\mathrm B}=(3.00-2.80)\,\mathrm{eV}=0.20\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

A and B emit. Metal C has W greater than 3.00 eV, so it does not emit.

Learning HubPage 18 / 23
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Examples 5–6 · Extract quantities from graphs

Example 5 · Planck constant from two graph points

For metal A, a frequency of 7.00 × 10¹⁴ Hz gives a maximum kinetic energy of 0.395 eV, and 9.00 × 10¹⁴ Hz gives 1.222 eV. Find the slope of the maximum kinetic energy versus frequency graph in eV·s, then use the first point to find the work function in eV.

Use equation (2)

\[\begin{aligned}h&=\frac{\Delta K_{\max}}{\Delta f}=\frac{1.222-0.395}{(9.00-7.00)\times10^{14}}=4.14\times10^{-15}\,\mathrm{eV\,s} \\ W&=hf-K_{\max}=(4.135\times10^{-15})(7.00\times10^{14})-0.395\approx2.50\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Retain the unrounded slope when calculating W.

Example 6 · Use the teaching I–V graph to find the work function

With 3.00 eV photons, the teaching graph shows 0.20 µA at a collector voltage of −0.45 V, and a first zero reading of 0.00 µA at −0.50 V. Estimate the stopping-potential magnitude in V, then find the maximum kinetic energy and work function in eV.

Use equation (4), (2)

\[\begin{aligned}V&\approx-0.50\,\mathrm V,\qquad V_s\approx0.50\,\mathrm V \\ K_{\max}&=eV_s\approx(1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm C)(0.50\,\mathrm V)=8.01\times10^{-20}\,\mathrm J=0.50\,\mathrm{eV} \\ W&=E_\gamma-K_{\max}\approx3.00-0.50=2.50\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

The measurements bracket 0.45 < Vₛ ≤ 0.50 V. The calculation uses the first zero as an estimate.

Learning HubPage 19 / 23
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Example 7 · From your readings to a stopping-potential graph

Return to the lab, choose “Explore the tube”, and use the three frequency presets for metal A at 50% intensity. Record the last voltage with current and the first zero-current voltage, 0.01 V apart.

f (10¹⁴ Hz)Last V with I > 0 (V)First V with I = 0 (V)Vₛ (V)
6.50−0.18−0.190.19
7.25−0.49−0.500.50
8.00−0.80−0.810.81

Apply equation (5) to the Vₛ values in the table.

Plot the measured points, draw a trend line and extend it with dashes to read the intercepts. The measured points are above threshold.

Use equation (5)

\[\begin{aligned}\frac{h}{e}&\approx\frac{0.81-0.19}{(8.00-6.50)\times10^{14}}=4.13\times10^{-15}\,\mathrm{V\,s} \\ h&=e\frac{h}{e}\approx(1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm C)(4.13\times10^{-15}\,\mathrm{V\,s})\approx6.62\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s} \\ f_0&\approx6.04\times10^{14}\,\mathrm{Hz} \\ -\frac{W}{e}&\approx-2.50\,\mathrm V\quad\Rightarrow\quad W\approx2.50\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Changing the metal keeps the slope the same and shifts the intercepts according to W.

Examples 8–9 · Speed and particle counts

Example 8 · From stopping potential to speed

The lab gives a stopping potential of about 0.50 V for metal A. Find the maximum electron speed. The electron mass is 9.11 × 10⁻³¹ kg.

Use equation (4), (6)

\[\begin{aligned}K_{\max}&=eV_s=(1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm C)(0.50\,\mathrm V)=8.01\times10^{-20}\,\mathrm J \\ v_{\max}&=\sqrt{\frac{2K_{\max}}{m_e}}\approx\sqrt{\frac{2(8.01\times10^{-20}\,\mathrm J)}{9.11\times10^{-31}\,\mathrm{kg}}}\approx4.19\times10^5\,\mathrm{m/s}\end{aligned}\]

Use kinetic energy in joules to obtain speed in m/s.

Example 9 · From light intensity to photon count

Light of intensity 0.20 W/m² is incident normally on 2.0 cm² for 5.0 s. Each photon carries 4.0 × 10⁻¹⁹ J. Find the incident photon count. If every photon is absorbed and releases one electron, how many electrons are emitted?

Use equation (7), (8)

\[\begin{aligned}E_{\mathrm{total}}&=I_{\mathrm{light}} A\Delta t=(0.20\,\mathrm{W/m^2})(2.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2})(5.0\,\mathrm s)=2.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm J \\ N&=\frac{E_{\mathrm{total}}}{E_\gamma}=\frac{2.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm J}{4.0\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J}=5.0\times10^{14}\end{aligned}\]

The photon and electron counts are equal under the stated assumption.

Learning HubPage 21 / 23
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Example 10 · One light source, two metals

Example 10 · Use the first metal’s threshold to find the second metal’s work function

Shine light at the threshold frequency of sample metal X, whose work function is 3.60 eV, onto metal Y. The measured stopping potential is 0.90 V. Find the work function of Y.

Use equation (3), (4), (2)

\[\begin{aligned}E_\gamma&=hf_{0,X}=W_X=3.60\,\mathrm{eV} \\ K_{\max,Y}&=eV_s=0.90\,\mathrm{eV} \\ W_Y&=E_\gamma-K_{\max,Y}=(3.60-0.90)\,\mathrm{eV}=2.70\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

The light frequency stays the same when the metal changes.

Mission 1 · Use metal A and a collector voltage of +1.00 V. Light off: 0.00 µA. Teaching light at 3.00 eV and 50% intensity: 2.00 µA.

What do the readings before and after switching on the light show?

0.00 → 2.00 µA. The measured current shows that electrons travelled from the metal to the collector.

Learning HubPage 1 / 13
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Energy of a single photon

In the photon model, an electron at the metal can absorb all the energy of one photon. The delivered energy depends on the light frequency.

SymbolMeaning
\(E_\gamma\)Energy per photon, in J or eV
\(f\)Light frequency, in Hz
\(\lambda\)Wavelength in vacuum, in m
\(h\)Planck constant · \(6.626\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s}\)
\(c\)Speed of light in vacuum · \(3.00\times10^8\,\mathrm{m/s}\)

Quantum theory gives energy proportional to frequency. In vacuum, \(c=f\lambda\), so frequency is light speed divided by wavelength.

\(E_\gamma=hf=\frac{hc}{\lambda}\)(1)

\(1\,\mathrm{eV}=1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\qquad 1\,\mathrm{nm}=10^{-9}\,\mathrm m\)

At fixed frequency, how does raising intensity change the energy per photon and the number of photons incident per second?

Energy per photon is unchanged by equation (1). More photons arrive each second over the same illuminated area.

Learning HubPage 2 / 13
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Work function and the emission threshold

The work function \(W\) is the minimum energy required for an electron to escape the metal surface. It depends on the metal and surface condition. The remaining energy is kinetic. Electrons requiring the least escape energy have the maximum kinetic energy \(K_{\max}\). With consistent units, energy conservation gives \(hf=W+K_{\max}\).

\(K_{\max}=hf-W\)(2)

Let \(f_0\) be the threshold frequency. At the threshold, the photon energy just matches the work function, and equation (2) gives zero maximum kinetic energy in the ideal limit.

\(W=hf_0\)(3)
Light frequencyEmission from the metal
\(f\lt f_0\)The photon energy is insufficient. No photoelectrons are emitted.
\(f=f_0\)Ideal threshold: the energy just suffices and maximum kinetic energy is zero. The lab displays a current once frequency exceeds the threshold.
\(f>f_0\)Emission is possible and maximum kinetic energy is positive.

In the table on the first page, why does the meter still read 0.00 µA at 6.00 × 10¹⁴ Hz when intensity rises from 50% to 80%?

More photons arrive, but by equation (1) each still carries the same energy, below the work function in equation (3).

Learning HubPage 3 / 13
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Metal thresholds and the energy graph

Mission 4 · Compare your class readings with the model table for three metals at 50% intensity and +1.00 V collector voltage.

Frequency (10¹⁴ Hz)A (µA)B (µA)C (µA)
6.402.000.000.00
7.102.002.000.00
7.602.002.002.00

Why can changing the metal at the same frequency reduce the current to zero?

At 6.40, A gives a current. At 7.10, B gives a current when compared with C. At 7.60, C gives a current. By equation (3), larger W requires larger f₀.

Compare equation (2) with a straight-line equation. Frequency is on the horizontal axis and maximum kinetic energy is on the vertical axis. All slopes equal h in consistent units. Each horizontal intercept is f₀. Extending a line to f = 0 gives a vertical intercept of −W.

Graph-reading reference · 2025 A-Level Physics, question 22

Learning HubPage 4 / 13
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

From emission to the meter reading

The lab defines \(V=V_{\mathrm{collector}}-V_{\mathrm{emitter}}\). Positive V makes the collector attract electrons. With negative V, the electric field slows electrons moving toward the collector.

The electron has charge −e, where e is the elementary charge magnitude. Its electric potential energy changes by \(\Delta U=(-e)V\). When retarding voltage just brings the current to zero, let \(V_s\) be the stopping-potential magnitude. The signed axis reading is \(V=-V_s\). The fastest electrons need \(eV_s\) of energy to overcome the retarding field and reach the collection threshold.

\(K_{\max}=eV_s\)(4)

Use e = 1.602 × 10⁻¹⁹ C to answer in joules. For one electron, the numerical value of the energy in eV equals the numerical stopping-potential magnitude in volts.

The meter reads zero at 6.00 × 10¹⁴ Hz in Mission 2 and under teaching light at V = −0.50 V in Mission 3. What is different in the tube?

In Mission 2, the frequency is too low to emit electrons. In Mission 3, the light still causes emission, but the retarding voltage prevents electrons from reaching the collector.

Which interval is marked by the last nonzero reading and the first zero? Distinguish signed V from the magnitude Vₛ.

At −0.45 V the current is 0.20 µA. At −0.50 V it is zero. The two samples bracket 0.45 < Vₛ ≤ 0.50 V. The first zero gives V ≈ −0.50 V or Vₛ ≈ 0.50 V, which equals the exact model cutoff for this preset.

Learning HubPage 6 / 13
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

From readings to a graph, speed and photon count

Vₛ is the stopping-potential magnitude and V is the collector voltage relative to the emitter. When sweeping toward negative voltage, estimate Vₛ as −V at the first zero-current reading.

Substitute the kinetic energy from equation (2) into (4), then divide by the electron charge magnitude e.

\(eV_s=hf-W\)

\(V_s=\frac{h}{e}f-\frac{W}{e}\)(5)

On the Vₛ versus f graph, the slope is h/e, the horizontal intercept is f₀, and the extrapolated vertical intercept is −W/e.

Let mₑ be the electron mass in kg and vₘₐₓ the maximum speed in m/s. Use the nonrelativistic kinetic energy in joules.

\(K_{\max}=\frac{1}{2}m_ev_{\max}^2\)(6)

Let I_light be irradiance in W/m², A the illuminated area in m², and Δt the time interval in seconds. For normally incident light with constant irradiance, intensity is total energy per area per time.

\(I_{\mathrm{light}}=\frac{E_{\mathrm{total}}}{A\Delta t}\)

\(E_{\mathrm{total}}=I_{\mathrm{light}}A\Delta t\)(7)

If every photon carries the same energy Eγ, let N be the incident photon count. The total energy is the sum of the energies of all N photons.

\(E_{\mathrm{total}}=NE_\gamma\)

\(N=\frac{E_{\mathrm{total}}}{E_\gamma}\)(8)
Learning HubPage 7 / 13
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Examples 1–2 · Calculations from photon energy

Calculation data · \(h=6.626\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s}\) · \(c=3.00\times10^8\,\mathrm{m/s}\) · \(1\,\mathrm{eV}=1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\) · \(1\,\mathrm{nm}=10^{-9}\,\mathrm m\)

Example 1 · Photon energy

Light in vacuum has a wavelength of 413.3 nm. Calculate the energy of one photon in joules and electronvolts.

Use equation (1)

\[\begin{aligned}\lambda&=413.3\times10^{-9}\,\mathrm m \\ E_\gamma&=\frac{hc}{\lambda}=\frac{(6.626\times10^{-34})(3.00\times10^8)}{413.3\times10^{-9}}=4.81\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J \\ E_\gamma&=\frac{4.81\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J}{1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm{J/eV}}\approx3.00\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Each photon carries about 3.00 eV.

Example 2 · Work function from threshold frequency

Metal A has a threshold frequency of about 6.04 × 10¹⁴ Hz. Find the work function in eV and the maximum kinetic energy under 3.00 eV photons.

Use equation (3), (2)

\[\begin{aligned}W&=hf_0=(6.626\times10^{-34})(6.04\times10^{14})=4.00\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\approx2.50\,\mathrm{eV} \\ K_{\max}&=E_\gamma-W=(3.00-2.50)\,\mathrm{eV}=0.50\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Photon energy exceeds W, so emission occurs.

Learning HubPage 8 / 13
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Examples 3–4 · Check emission and compare metals

Calculation data · \(h=6.626\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s}\) · \(c=3.00\times10^8\,\mathrm{m/s}\) · \(1\,\mathrm{eV}=1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\) · \(1\,\mathrm{nm}=10^{-9}\,\mathrm m\)

Example 3 · A wavelength beyond threshold

Shine 600 nm light on metal A, whose work function is 2.50 eV. Decide whether electrons are emitted. If there is no emission, how much more energy per photon is needed to reach the threshold?

Use equation (1)

\[\begin{aligned}E_\gamma&=\frac{hc}{\lambda}=\frac{(6.626\times10^{-34})(3.00\times10^8)}{600\times10^{-9}}=3.31\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\approx2.07\,\mathrm{eV} \\ W-E_\gamma&\approx2.50-2.07=0.43\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

There is no emission. Each photon needs about 0.43 eV more to reach the threshold.

Example 4 · One light setting, three metals

Shine 3.00 eV photons on metals A, B and C, whose work functions are 2.50, 2.80 and 3.10 eV, respectively. Which metals emit electrons, and what is the maximum kinetic energy in eV for each emitting metal?

Use equation (2)

\[\begin{aligned}K_{\max,\mathrm A}&=E_\gamma-W_{\mathrm A}=(3.00-2.50)\,\mathrm{eV}=0.50\,\mathrm{eV} \\ K_{\max,\mathrm B}&=E_\gamma-W_{\mathrm B}=(3.00-2.80)\,\mathrm{eV}=0.20\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

A and B emit. Metal C has W greater than 3.00 eV, so it does not emit.

Learning HubPage 9 / 13
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Examples 5–6 · Extract quantities from graphs

Example 5 · Planck constant from two graph points

For metal A, a frequency of 7.00 × 10¹⁴ Hz gives a maximum kinetic energy of 0.395 eV, and 9.00 × 10¹⁴ Hz gives 1.222 eV. Find the slope of the maximum kinetic energy versus frequency graph in eV·s, then use the first point to find the work function in eV.

Use equation (2)

\[\begin{aligned}h&=\frac{\Delta K_{\max}}{\Delta f}=\frac{1.222-0.395}{(9.00-7.00)\times10^{14}}=4.14\times10^{-15}\,\mathrm{eV\,s} \\ W&=hf-K_{\max}=(4.135\times10^{-15})(7.00\times10^{14})-0.395\approx2.50\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Retain the unrounded slope when calculating W.

Example 6 · Use the teaching I–V graph to find the work function

With 3.00 eV photons, the teaching graph shows 0.20 µA at a collector voltage of −0.45 V, and a first zero reading of 0.00 µA at −0.50 V. Estimate the stopping-potential magnitude in V, then find the maximum kinetic energy and work function in eV.

Use equation (4), (2)

\[\begin{aligned}V&\approx-0.50\,\mathrm V,\qquad V_s\approx0.50\,\mathrm V \\ K_{\max}&=eV_s\approx(1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm C)(0.50\,\mathrm V)=8.01\times10^{-20}\,\mathrm J=0.50\,\mathrm{eV} \\ W&=E_\gamma-K_{\max}\approx3.00-0.50=2.50\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

The measurements bracket 0.45 < Vₛ ≤ 0.50 V. The calculation uses the first zero as an estimate.

PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Example 7 · From your readings to a stopping-potential graph

Return to the lab, choose “Explore the tube”, and use the three frequency presets for metal A at 50% intensity. Record the last voltage with current and the first zero-current voltage, 0.01 V apart.

f (10¹⁴ Hz)Last V with I > 0 (V)First V with I = 0 (V)Vₛ (V)
6.50−0.18−0.190.19
7.25−0.49−0.500.50
8.00−0.80−0.810.81

Apply equation (5) to the Vₛ values in the table.

Plot the measured points, draw a trend line and extend it with dashes to read the intercepts. The measured points are above threshold.

Use equation (5)

\[\begin{aligned}\frac{h}{e}&\approx\frac{0.81-0.19}{(8.00-6.50)\times10^{14}}=4.13\times10^{-15}\,\mathrm{V\,s} \\ h&=e\frac{h}{e}\approx(1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm C)(4.13\times10^{-15}\,\mathrm{V\,s})\approx6.62\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s} \\ f_0&\approx6.04\times10^{14}\,\mathrm{Hz} \\ -\frac{W}{e}&\approx-2.50\,\mathrm V\quad\Rightarrow\quad W\approx2.50\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Changing the metal keeps the slope the same and shifts the intercepts according to W.

Learning HubPage 11 / 13
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Examples 8–9 · Speed and particle counts

Example 8 · From stopping potential to speed

The lab gives a stopping potential of about 0.50 V for metal A. Find the maximum electron speed. The electron mass is 9.11 × 10⁻³¹ kg.

Use equation (4), (6)

\[\begin{aligned}K_{\max}&=eV_s=(1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm C)(0.50\,\mathrm V)=8.01\times10^{-20}\,\mathrm J \\ v_{\max}&=\sqrt{\frac{2K_{\max}}{m_e}}\approx\sqrt{\frac{2(8.01\times10^{-20}\,\mathrm J)}{9.11\times10^{-31}\,\mathrm{kg}}}\approx4.19\times10^5\,\mathrm{m/s}\end{aligned}\]

Use kinetic energy in joules to obtain speed in m/s.

Example 9 · From light intensity to photon count

Light of intensity 0.20 W/m² is incident normally on 2.0 cm² for 5.0 s. Each photon carries 4.0 × 10⁻¹⁹ J. Find the incident photon count. If every photon is absorbed and releases one electron, how many electrons are emitted?

Use equation (7), (8)

\[\begin{aligned}E_{\mathrm{total}}&=I_{\mathrm{light}} A\Delta t=(0.20\,\mathrm{W/m^2})(2.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2})(5.0\,\mathrm s)=2.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm J \\ N&=\frac{E_{\mathrm{total}}}{E_\gamma}=\frac{2.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm J}{4.0\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J}=5.0\times10^{14}\end{aligned}\]

The photon and electron counts are equal under the stated assumption.

Learning HubPage 12 / 13
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Example 10 · One light source, two metals

Example 10 · Use the first metal’s threshold to find the second metal’s work function

Shine light at the threshold frequency of sample metal X, whose work function is 3.60 eV, onto metal Y. The measured stopping potential is 0.90 V. Find the work function of Y.

Use equation (3), (4), (2)

\[\begin{aligned}E_\gamma&=hf_{0,X}=W_X=3.60\,\mathrm{eV} \\ K_{\max,Y}&=eV_s=0.90\,\mathrm{eV} \\ W_Y&=E_\gamma-K_{\max,Y}=(3.60-0.90)\,\mathrm{eV}=2.70\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

The light frequency stays the same when the metal changes.

Name Class

Passage A · Which light can release electrons?

Choose words or sentences from the banks below and write them in the blanks. Some choices are distractors.

Word Bank

  • work function
  • intensity
  • photons
  • threshold frequency
  • electric current
  • resistance
  • frequency

Sentence Bank

  1. Energy per photon stays the same, while more photons arrive each second.
  2. Increasing intensity always increases energy per photon.
  3. Below threshold, the current remains zero even when intensity increases.

When the microammeter detects electric current with the light on, electrons from the metal have reached the collector. Light delivers energy in packets called photons. The minimum energy needed for an electron to leave the surface is the work function, and the frequency where photon energy equals that amount is the threshold frequency.

Consider light above threshold. Explain what happens to the photons when intensity increases at fixed frequency.

Energy per photon stays the same, while more photons arrive each second.

Repeat below threshold. What happens to the meter reading when intensity increases?

Below threshold, the current remains zero even when intensity increases.

Passage B · How do we measure maximum kinetic energy?

Choose words or sentences from the banks below and write them in the blanks. Some choices are distractors.

Word Bank

  • emitter
  • collector
  • electric field
  • stopping potential
  • saturation current
  • wavelength
  • decreases

Sentence Bank

  1. Electrons still leave the metal, but none reaches the collector.
  2. Photon energy decreases as retarding voltage increases.
  3. Multiply the stopping-potential magnitude by the magnitude of the electron charge.

Above threshold, electrons leave the emitter and travel toward the collector. Lowering the collector voltage makes the electric field oppose their motion. As the retarding voltage increases, the measured current decreases. The smallest retarding voltage magnitude that brings current to zero is the stopping potential.

What happens in the tube when the meter first reads zero at this setting?

Electrons still leave the metal, but none reaches the collector.

How do you use the voltage reading to find the maximum kinetic energy?

Multiply the stopping-potential magnitude by the magnitude of the electron charge.

1. Photon energy and unit conversion

A beam of light travels through a vacuum and has a wavelength of 500 nm. Use the following constants and conversion factor. \(c=3.00\times10^8\,\mathrm{m/s}\) · \(h=6.626\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s}=4.136\times10^{-15}\,\mathrm{eV\,s}\) · \(1\,\mathrm{eV}=1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\)

a. Find the photon energy in joules.

Use equation (1)

\[\begin{aligned}\lambda&=500\times10^{-9}\,\mathrm m \\ E_\gamma&=\frac{hc}{\lambda}=\frac{(6.626\times10^{-34})(3.00\times10^8)}{500\times10^{-9}}=3.98\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\end{aligned}\]

The photon energy is about 3.98 × 10⁻¹⁹ J.

b. Convert the energy to electronvolts.

Use equation (1)

\[\begin{aligned}E_\gamma&=\frac{3.9756\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J}{1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm{J/eV}}=2.48\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Use the unrounded value from part a.

2. Two frequencies on the same metal

Metal B in the lab has a work function of 2.80 eV. A student illuminates it at 6.50 × 10¹⁴ Hz, then repeats at 7.80 × 10¹⁴ Hz. Use the Planck constant \(h=6.626\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s}=4.136\times10^{-15}\,\mathrm{eV\,s}\).

a. Decide whether each beam produces emission and support the decision using energy.

Use equation (1)

\[\begin{aligned}E_{\gamma,1}&=hf_1=(4.136\times10^{-15})(6.50\times10^{14})=2.69\,\mathrm{eV} \\ E_{\gamma,2}&=hf_2=(4.136\times10^{-15})(7.80\times10^{14})=3.23\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

The first beam is below 2.80 eV, so it produces no emission. The second beam is above 2.80 eV, so emission occurs.

b. Find maximum kinetic energy for the emitting case.

Use equation (2)

\[\begin{aligned}K_{\max}&=E_\gamma-W=(3.22608-2.80)\,\mathrm{eV}\approx0.43\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Use the photon energy of the higher-frequency beam.

4. Three readings of zero

Students use metal A, which has a work function of 2.50 eV. They change the light and collector voltage as shown. The meter reads 0.00 µA in all three cases.

CaseLightV (V)
AOff+1.00
BOn · 2.40 eV+1.00
COn · 3.00 eV−0.60

a. Case A Are electrons emitted? Explain the zero reading.

The source is off. No incident photons trigger emission.

b. Case B Are electrons emitted? Explain the zero reading.

Each 2.40 eV photon is below 2.50 eV, so no electron is emitted.

c. In case C, are electrons emitted? If the light is kept fixed and the collector voltage is changed from −0.60 V to +1.00 V, will the meter detect current? Explain.

Use equation (2)

\[\begin{aligned}K_{\max}&=E_\gamma-W=(3.00-2.50)\,\mathrm{eV}=0.50\,\mathrm{eV} \\ e|V|&=e(0.60\,\mathrm V)=0.60\,\mathrm{eV}\gt K_{\max}\end{aligned}\]

Photons cause emission. The −0.60 V setting requires 0.60 eV to reach the collector, so the current is initially zero. At +1.00 V the collector attracts the emitted electrons, and a current is measured.

5. Use an I–V graph to find the work function

A student shines 3.50 eV photons on a metal, then gradually lowers the collector voltage relative to the emitter and records the current shown.

b. Find maximum kinetic energy and work function in eV.

Use equation (4), (2)

\[\begin{aligned}K_{\max}&\approx e(0.70\,\mathrm V)=0.70\,\mathrm{eV} \\ W&=E_\gamma-K_{\max}\approx3.50-0.70=2.80\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Use the positive magnitude Vₛ.

7. A-Level 2024 · Question 23 (2567)

Light of energy 4.0 eV is incident on a sample metal plate. The electrons emitted have a maximum kinetic energy of 1.80 eV. If the light energy is changed to 2.0 eV, what is the maximum kinetic energy of the emitted electrons?

  1. No electrons are emitted from the metal plate.
  2. 0.6
  3. 1.1
  4. 1.8
  5. 2.2

Original exam question

Use equation (2)

\[\begin{aligned}W&=E_{\gamma,1}-K_{\max,1}=4.0\,\mathrm{eV}-1.80\,\mathrm{eV}=2.2\,\mathrm{eV} \\ E_{\gamma,2}&=2.0\,\mathrm{eV}\lt W=2.2\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Choice 1. The photon energy is too low for emission. The kinetic-energy equation applies when emission occurs. Subtracting here gives −0.2 eV, which indicates an energy shortfall.

8. A-Level 2025 · Set 1, Question 22 (2568)

A photoelectric experiment gives the straight-line graph shown for the maximum kinetic energy of the photoelectrons against the frequency of the light.

The horizontal and vertical intercepts are (x, 0) and (0, −y), where x and y are positive constants.

What are the Planck constant and the work function of the metal used in the experiment, respectively?

  1. \(x\) and \(y\)
  2. \(\tfrac{x}{y}\) and \(x\)
  3. \(\tfrac{x}{y}\) and \(y\)
  4. \(\tfrac{y}{x}\) and \(x\)
  5. \(\tfrac{y}{x}\) and \(y\)

Original exam question

Use equation (2)

\[\begin{aligned}h&=\frac{\Delta K_{\max}}{\Delta f}=\frac{0-(-y)}{x-0}=\frac{y}{x} \\ -W&=-y\quad\Rightarrow\quad W=y \\ W&=hf_0=\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)x=y\end{aligned}\]

Choice 5 follows from the slope and intercept. Equation (3) provides the check.

9. Nine Common Subjects 2022 · Question 23 (2565)

Electromagnetic waves at different frequencies are incident on the surface of a metal. The stopping potential is plotted against frequency as shown.

Let e be the electron charge and h the Planck constant in joule-seconds.

At frequency f, what is the maximum kinetic energy of the photoelectrons in electronvolts?

  1. \(\tfrac{hf}{e}-2\)
  2. \(\tfrac{hf}{e}+2\)
  3. \(\tfrac{hf}{e}+5\)
  4. \(hf-2e\)
  5. \(hf+2e\)

Original exam question

Use equation (2), (4)

\[\begin{aligned}eV_s&=hf-W\quad\Rightarrow\quad V_s=\frac{h}{e}f-\frac{W}{e} \\ -\frac{W}{e}&=-2\,\mathrm V\quad\Rightarrow\quad W=e(2\,\mathrm V)=2\,\mathrm{eV} \\ \frac{K_{\max}}{1\,\mathrm{eV}}&=\frac{hf}{1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J}-2\end{aligned}\]

Choice 1. The derivation uses e as the magnitude of the electron charge. With numerical SI values, dividing by the numerical value of e converts joules to electronvolts, giving hf/e − 2.

The −2 V intercept comes from extrapolating to f = 0. Vₛ in equation (4) is the stopping-potential magnitude in the emission regime. At cutoff on the simulation’s I–V graph, the signed voltage V equals −Vₛ.

10. Light intensity and particle counts

Light of intensity 0.30 W/m² is incident normally on 4.0 cm² for 2.0 s. Each photon carries 6.0 × 10⁻¹⁹ J. Assume every photon is absorbed and releases one electron.

a. Find the total incident light energy and the number of electrons emitted.

Use equation (7), (8)

\[\begin{aligned}E_{\mathrm{total}}&=I_{\mathrm{light}}A\Delta t=(0.30\,\mathrm{W/m^2})(4.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2})(2.0\,\mathrm s)=2.4\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm J \\ N&=\frac{E_{\mathrm{total}}}{E_\gamma}=\frac{2.4\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm J}{6.0\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J}=4.0\times10^{14}\end{aligned}\]

Use the area in square metres.

11. One light source, two metals

Light at the threshold frequency of metal X, whose work function is 4.00 eV, gives a stopping potential of 1.60 V when it shines on metal Y.

a. Find the photon energy and the work function of metal Y.

Use equation (3), (4), (2)

\[\begin{aligned}E_\gamma&=W_X=4.00\,\mathrm{eV} \\ K_{\max,Y}&=eV_s=1.60\,\mathrm{eV} \\ W_Y&=E_\gamma-K_{\max,Y}=(4.00-1.60)\,\mathrm{eV}=2.40\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

The same light delivers the same energy per photon to either metal.

Energy of a single photon

In the photon model, an electron at the metal can absorb all the energy of one photon. The delivered energy depends on the light frequency.

SymbolMeaning
\(E_\gamma\)Energy per photon, in J or eV
\(f\)Light frequency, in Hz
\(\lambda\)Wavelength in vacuum, in m
\(h\)Planck constant · \(6.626\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s}\)
\(c\)Speed of light in vacuum · \(3.00\times10^8\,\mathrm{m/s}\)

Quantum theory gives energy proportional to frequency. In vacuum, \(c=f\lambda\), so frequency is light speed divided by wavelength.

\(E_\gamma=hf=\frac{hc}{\lambda}\)(1)

\(1\,\mathrm{eV}=1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\qquad 1\,\mathrm{nm}=10^{-9}\,\mathrm m\)

At fixed frequency, how does raising intensity change the energy per photon and the number of photons incident per second?

Energy per photon is unchanged by equation (1). More photons arrive each second over the same illuminated area.

Learning HubPage 14 / 26
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Work function and the emission threshold

The work function \(W\) is the minimum energy required for an electron to escape the metal surface. It depends on the metal and surface condition. The remaining energy is kinetic. Electrons requiring the least escape energy have the maximum kinetic energy \(K_{\max}\). With consistent units, energy conservation gives \(hf=W+K_{\max}\).

\(K_{\max}=hf-W\)(2)

Let \(f_0\) be the threshold frequency. At the threshold, the photon energy just matches the work function, and equation (2) gives zero maximum kinetic energy in the ideal limit.

\(W=hf_0\)(3)
Light frequencyEmission from the metal
\(f\lt f_0\)The photon energy is insufficient. No photoelectrons are emitted.
\(f=f_0\)Ideal threshold: the energy just suffices and maximum kinetic energy is zero. The lab displays a current once frequency exceeds the threshold.
\(f>f_0\)Emission is possible and maximum kinetic energy is positive.

In the table on the first page, why does the meter still read 0.00 µA at 6.00 × 10¹⁴ Hz when intensity rises from 50% to 80%?

More photons arrive, but by equation (1) each still carries the same energy, below the work function in equation (3).

PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Metal thresholds and the energy graph

Mission 4 · Compare your class readings with the model table for three metals at 50% intensity and +1.00 V collector voltage.

Frequency (10¹⁴ Hz)A (µA)B (µA)C (µA)
6.402.000.000.00
7.102.002.000.00
7.602.002.002.00

Why can changing the metal at the same frequency reduce the current to zero?

At 6.40, A gives a current. At 7.10, B gives a current when compared with C. At 7.60, C gives a current. By equation (3), larger W requires larger f₀.

Compare equation (2) with a straight-line equation. Frequency is on the horizontal axis and maximum kinetic energy is on the vertical axis. All slopes equal h in consistent units. Each horizontal intercept is f₀. Extending a line to f = 0 gives a vertical intercept of −W.

From emission to the meter reading

The lab defines \(V=V_{\mathrm{collector}}-V_{\mathrm{emitter}}\). Positive V makes the collector attract electrons. With negative V, the electric field slows electrons moving toward the collector.

The electron has charge −e, where e is the elementary charge magnitude. Its electric potential energy changes by \(\Delta U=(-e)V\). When retarding voltage just brings the current to zero, let \(V_s\) be the stopping-potential magnitude. The signed axis reading is \(V=-V_s\). The fastest electrons need \(eV_s\) of energy to overcome the retarding field and reach the collection threshold.

\(K_{\max}=eV_s\)(4)

Use e = 1.602 × 10⁻¹⁹ C to answer in joules. For one electron, the numerical value of the energy in eV equals the numerical stopping-potential magnitude in volts.

The meter reads zero at 6.00 × 10¹⁴ Hz in Mission 2 and under teaching light at V = −0.50 V in Mission 3. What is different in the tube?

In Mission 2, the frequency is too low to emit electrons. In Mission 3, the light still causes emission, but the retarding voltage prevents electrons from reaching the collector.

From readings to a graph, speed and photon count

Vₛ is the stopping-potential magnitude and V is the collector voltage relative to the emitter. When sweeping toward negative voltage, estimate Vₛ as −V at the first zero-current reading.

Substitute the kinetic energy from equation (2) into (4), then divide by the electron charge magnitude e.

\(eV_s=hf-W\)

\(V_s=\frac{h}{e}f-\frac{W}{e}\)(5)

On the Vₛ versus f graph, the slope is h/e, the horizontal intercept is f₀, and the extrapolated vertical intercept is −W/e.

Let mₑ be the electron mass in kg and vₘₐₓ the maximum speed in m/s. Use the nonrelativistic kinetic energy in joules.

\(K_{\max}=\frac{1}{2}m_ev_{\max}^2\)(6)

Let I_light be irradiance in W/m², A the illuminated area in m², and Δt the time interval in seconds. For normally incident light with constant irradiance, intensity is total energy per area per time.

\(I_{\mathrm{light}}=\frac{E_{\mathrm{total}}}{A\Delta t}\)

\(E_{\mathrm{total}}=I_{\mathrm{light}}A\Delta t\)(7)

If every photon carries the same energy Eγ, let N be the incident photon count. The total energy is the sum of the energies of all N photons.

\(E_{\mathrm{total}}=NE_\gamma\)

\(N=\frac{E_{\mathrm{total}}}{E_\gamma}\)(8)

PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Examples 1–2 · Calculations from photon energy

Calculation data · \(h=6.626\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s}\) · \(c=3.00\times10^8\,\mathrm{m/s}\) · \(1\,\mathrm{eV}=1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\) · \(1\,\mathrm{nm}=10^{-9}\,\mathrm m\)

Example 1 · Photon energy

Light in vacuum has a wavelength of 413.3 nm. Calculate the energy of one photon in joules and electronvolts.

Use equation (1)

\[\begin{aligned}\lambda&=413.3\times10^{-9}\,\mathrm m \\ E_\gamma&=\frac{hc}{\lambda}=\frac{(6.626\times10^{-34})(3.00\times10^8)}{413.3\times10^{-9}}=4.81\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J \\ E_\gamma&=\frac{4.81\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J}{1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm{J/eV}}\approx3.00\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Each photon carries about 3.00 eV.

Example 2 · Work function from threshold frequency

Metal A has a threshold frequency of about 6.04 × 10¹⁴ Hz. Find the work function in eV and the maximum kinetic energy under 3.00 eV photons.

Use equation (3), (2)

\[\begin{aligned}W&=hf_0=(6.626\times10^{-34})(6.04\times10^{14})=4.00\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\approx2.50\,\mathrm{eV} \\ K_{\max}&=E_\gamma-W=(3.00-2.50)\,\mathrm{eV}=0.50\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Photon energy exceeds W, so emission occurs.

Learning HubPage 20 / 26
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Examples 3–4 · Check emission and compare metals

Calculation data · \(h=6.626\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s}\) · \(c=3.00\times10^8\,\mathrm{m/s}\) · \(1\,\mathrm{eV}=1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\) · \(1\,\mathrm{nm}=10^{-9}\,\mathrm m\)

Example 3 · A wavelength beyond threshold

Shine 600 nm light on metal A, whose work function is 2.50 eV. Decide whether electrons are emitted. If there is no emission, how much more energy per photon is needed to reach the threshold?

Use equation (1)

\[\begin{aligned}E_\gamma&=\frac{hc}{\lambda}=\frac{(6.626\times10^{-34})(3.00\times10^8)}{600\times10^{-9}}=3.31\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\approx2.07\,\mathrm{eV} \\ W-E_\gamma&\approx2.50-2.07=0.43\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

There is no emission. Each photon needs about 0.43 eV more to reach the threshold.

Example 4 · One light setting, three metals

Shine 3.00 eV photons on metals A, B and C, whose work functions are 2.50, 2.80 and 3.10 eV, respectively. Which metals emit electrons, and what is the maximum kinetic energy in eV for each emitting metal?

Use equation (2)

\[\begin{aligned}K_{\max,\mathrm A}&=E_\gamma-W_{\mathrm A}=(3.00-2.50)\,\mathrm{eV}=0.50\,\mathrm{eV} \\ K_{\max,\mathrm B}&=E_\gamma-W_{\mathrm B}=(3.00-2.80)\,\mathrm{eV}=0.20\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

A and B emit. Metal C has W greater than 3.00 eV, so it does not emit.

Learning HubPage 21 / 26
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Examples 5–6 · Extract quantities from graphs

Example 5 · Planck constant from two graph points

For metal A, a frequency of 7.00 × 10¹⁴ Hz gives a maximum kinetic energy of 0.395 eV, and 9.00 × 10¹⁴ Hz gives 1.222 eV. Find the slope of the maximum kinetic energy versus frequency graph in eV·s, then use the first point to find the work function in eV.

Use equation (2)

\[\begin{aligned}h&=\frac{\Delta K_{\max}}{\Delta f}=\frac{1.222-0.395}{(9.00-7.00)\times10^{14}}=4.14\times10^{-15}\,\mathrm{eV\,s} \\ W&=hf-K_{\max}=(4.135\times10^{-15})(7.00\times10^{14})-0.395\approx2.50\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Retain the unrounded slope when calculating W.

Example 6 · Use the teaching I–V graph to find the work function

With 3.00 eV photons, the teaching graph shows 0.20 µA at a collector voltage of −0.45 V, and a first zero reading of 0.00 µA at −0.50 V. Estimate the stopping-potential magnitude in V, then find the maximum kinetic energy and work function in eV.

Use equation (4), (2)

\[\begin{aligned}V&\approx-0.50\,\mathrm V,\qquad V_s\approx0.50\,\mathrm V \\ K_{\max}&=eV_s\approx(1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm C)(0.50\,\mathrm V)=8.01\times10^{-20}\,\mathrm J=0.50\,\mathrm{eV} \\ W&=E_\gamma-K_{\max}\approx3.00-0.50=2.50\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

The measurements bracket 0.45 < Vₛ ≤ 0.50 V. The calculation uses the first zero as an estimate.

Example 7 · From your readings to a stopping-potential graph

Return to the lab, choose “Explore the tube”, and use the three frequency presets for metal A at 50% intensity. Record the last voltage with current and the first zero-current voltage, 0.01 V apart.

f (10¹⁴ Hz)Last V with I > 0 (V)First V with I = 0 (V)Vₛ (V)
6.50−0.18−0.190.19
7.25−0.49−0.500.50
8.00−0.80−0.810.81

Apply equation (5) to the Vₛ values in the table.

Plot the measured points, draw a trend line and extend it with dashes to read the intercepts. The measured points are above threshold.

Use equation (5)

\[\begin{aligned}\frac{h}{e}&\approx\frac{0.81-0.19}{(8.00-6.50)\times10^{14}}=4.13\times10^{-15}\,\mathrm{V\,s} \\ h&=e\frac{h}{e}\approx(1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm C)(4.13\times10^{-15}\,\mathrm{V\,s})\approx6.62\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s} \\ f_0&\approx6.04\times10^{14}\,\mathrm{Hz} \\ -\frac{W}{e}&\approx-2.50\,\mathrm V\quad\Rightarrow\quad W\approx2.50\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Changing the metal keeps the slope the same and shifts the intercepts according to W.

Learning HubPage 23 / 26
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Examples 8–9 · Speed and particle counts

Example 8 · From stopping potential to speed

The lab gives a stopping potential of about 0.50 V for metal A. Find the maximum electron speed. The electron mass is 9.11 × 10⁻³¹ kg.

Use equation (4), (6)

\[\begin{aligned}K_{\max}&=eV_s=(1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm C)(0.50\,\mathrm V)=8.01\times10^{-20}\,\mathrm J \\ v_{\max}&=\sqrt{\frac{2K_{\max}}{m_e}}\approx\sqrt{\frac{2(8.01\times10^{-20}\,\mathrm J)}{9.11\times10^{-31}\,\mathrm{kg}}}\approx4.19\times10^5\,\mathrm{m/s}\end{aligned}\]

Use kinetic energy in joules to obtain speed in m/s.

Example 9 · From light intensity to photon count

Light of intensity 0.20 W/m² is incident normally on 2.0 cm² for 5.0 s. Each photon carries 4.0 × 10⁻¹⁹ J. Find the incident photon count. If every photon is absorbed and releases one electron, how many electrons are emitted?

Use equation (7), (8)

\[\begin{aligned}E_{\mathrm{total}}&=I_{\mathrm{light}} A\Delta t=(0.20\,\mathrm{W/m^2})(2.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2})(5.0\,\mathrm s)=2.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm J \\ N&=\frac{E_{\mathrm{total}}}{E_\gamma}=\frac{2.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm J}{4.0\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J}=5.0\times10^{14}\end{aligned}\]

The photon and electron counts are equal under the stated assumption.

Learning HubPage 24 / 26
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Example 10 · One light source, two metals

Example 10 · Use the first metal’s threshold to find the second metal’s work function

Shine light at the threshold frequency of sample metal X, whose work function is 3.60 eV, onto metal Y. The measured stopping potential is 0.90 V. Find the work function of Y.

Use equation (3), (4), (2)

\[\begin{aligned}E_\gamma&=hf_{0,X}=W_X=3.60\,\mathrm{eV} \\ K_{\max,Y}&=eV_s=0.90\,\mathrm{eV} \\ W_Y&=E_\gamma-K_{\max,Y}=(3.60-0.90)\,\mathrm{eV}=2.70\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

The light frequency stays the same when the metal changes.

Knowledge Recall

Cover or close the Notes and answer from memory.

1. What does the meter measure, and what does a nonzero reading tell you about electrons?

It measures circuit current. Electrons emitted from the metal have reached the collector.

2. State the below, at and above threshold cases. What does increasing intensity do at fixed frequency?

Below threshold there is no emission. At threshold Kₘₐₓ = 0 in the ideal limit. Above threshold Kₘₐₓ is positive. At fixed f, higher intensity raises photon rate and, above threshold with the same collecting setup, raises current.

3. Which settings should you check before deciding whether electrons were emitted when current is zero?

Check whether the light is on, whether f is above threshold, and whether retarding voltage stops electrons from reaching the collector.

4. When the current drops to zero, what sign does the collector voltage have? How can you use the stopping-potential magnitude to find the maximum kinetic energy?

At cutoff, V = −Vₛ and Kₘₐₓ = eVₛ.

5. On a graph of maximum kinetic energy against light frequency, what do the slope and the two intercepts represent?

The slope is h in consistent units. The horizontal intercept is f₀, and the extrapolated vertical intercept is −W.

The minimum energy needed for an electron to leave the surface is the work function, and the frequency where photon energy equals that amount is the threshold frequency.

Energy per photon stays the same, while more photons arrive each second.

Passage B · How do we measure maximum kinetic energy?

Choose words or sentences from the banks below and write them in the blanks. Some choices are distractors.

Word Bank

  • emitter
  • collector
  • electric field
  • stopping potential
  • saturation current

Sentence Bank

  1. Electrons still leave the metal, but none reaches the collector.
  2. Photon energy decreases as retarding voltage increases.
  3. Multiply the stopping-potential magnitude by the magnitude of the electron charge.

Above threshold, electrons leave the emitter and travel toward the collector. Lowering the collector voltage makes the electric field oppose their motion. As the retarding voltage increases, the measured current decreases. The smallest retarding voltage magnitude that brings current to zero is the stopping potential.

What happens in the tube when the meter first reads zero at this setting?

Electrons still leave the metal, but none reaches the collector.

How do you use the voltage reading to find the maximum kinetic energy?

Multiply the stopping-potential magnitude by the magnitude of the electron charge.

1. Photon energy and unit conversion

A beam of light travels through a vacuum and has a wavelength of 500 nm. Use the following constants and conversion factor. \(c=3.00\times10^8\,\mathrm{m/s}\) · \(h=6.626\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s}=4.136\times10^{-15}\,\mathrm{eV\,s}\) · \(1\,\mathrm{eV}=1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\)

a. Find the photon energy in joules.

Use equation (1)

\[\begin{aligned}\lambda&=500\times10^{-9}\,\mathrm m \\ E_\gamma&=\frac{hc}{\lambda}=\frac{(6.626\times10^{-34})(3.00\times10^8)}{500\times10^{-9}}=3.98\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\end{aligned}\]

The photon energy is about 3.98 × 10⁻¹⁹ J.

b. Convert the energy to electronvolts.

Use equation (1)

\[\begin{aligned}E_\gamma&=\frac{3.9756\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J}{1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm{J/eV}}=2.48\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Use the unrounded value from part a.

2. Two frequencies on the same metal

Metal B in the lab has a work function of 2.80 eV. A student illuminates it at 6.50 × 10¹⁴ Hz, then repeats at 7.80 × 10¹⁴ Hz. Use the Planck constant \(h=6.626\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s}=4.136\times10^{-15}\,\mathrm{eV\,s}\).

a. Decide whether each beam produces emission and support the decision using energy.

Use equation (1)

\[\begin{aligned}E_{\gamma,1}&=hf_1=(4.136\times10^{-15})(6.50\times10^{14})=2.69\,\mathrm{eV} \\ E_{\gamma,2}&=hf_2=(4.136\times10^{-15})(7.80\times10^{14})=3.23\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

The first beam is below 2.80 eV, so it produces no emission. The second beam is above 2.80 eV, so emission occurs.

b. Find maximum kinetic energy for the emitting case.

Use equation (2)

\[\begin{aligned}K_{\max}&=E_\gamma-W=(3.22608-2.80)\,\mathrm{eV}\approx0.43\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Use the photon energy of the higher-frequency beam.

4. Three readings of zero

Students use metal A, which has a work function of 2.50 eV. They change the light and collector voltage as shown. The meter reads 0.00 µA in all three cases.

CaseLightV (V)
AOff+1.00
BOn · 2.40 eV+1.00
COn · 3.00 eV−0.60

The source is off. No incident photons trigger emission.

b. Case B Are electrons emitted? Explain the zero reading.

Each 2.40 eV photon is below 2.50 eV, so no electron is emitted.

c. In case C, are electrons emitted? If the light is kept fixed and the collector voltage is changed from −0.60 V to +1.00 V, will the meter detect current? Explain.

\[\begin{aligned}K_{\max}&=E_\gamma-W=(3.00-2.50)\,\mathrm{eV}=0.50\,\mathrm{eV} \\ e|V|&=e(0.60\,\mathrm V)=0.60\,\mathrm{eV}\gt K_{\max}\end{aligned}\]

Photons cause emission. The −0.60 V setting requires 0.60 eV to reach the collector, so the current is initially zero. At +1.00 V the collector attracts the emitted electrons, and a current is measured.

5. Use an I–V graph to find the work function

A student shines 3.50 eV photons on a metal, then gradually lowers the collector voltage relative to the emitter and records the current shown.

7. A-Level 2024 · Question 23 (2567)

Light of energy 4.0 eV is incident on a sample metal plate. The electrons emitted have a maximum kinetic energy of 1.80 eV. If the light energy is changed to 2.0 eV, what is the maximum kinetic energy of the emitted electrons?

  1. No electrons are emitted from the metal plate.
  2. 0.6
  3. 1.1
  4. 1.8
  5. 2.2

Original exam question

Use equation (2)

\[\begin{aligned}W&=E_{\gamma,1}-K_{\max,1}=4.0\,\mathrm{eV}-1.80\,\mathrm{eV}=2.2\,\mathrm{eV} \\ E_{\gamma,2}&=2.0\,\mathrm{eV}\lt W=2.2\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Choice 1. The photon energy is too low for emission. The kinetic-energy equation applies when emission occurs. Subtracting here gives −0.2 eV, which indicates an energy shortfall.

PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectExit Ticket · Answers

8. A-Level 2025 · Set 1, Question 22 (2568)

A photoelectric experiment gives the straight-line graph shown for the maximum kinetic energy of the photoelectrons against the frequency of the light.

The horizontal and vertical intercepts are (x, 0) and (0, −y), where x and y are positive constants.

What are the Planck constant and the work function of the metal used in the experiment, respectively?

  1. \(x\) and \(y\)
  2. \(\tfrac{x}{y}\) and \(x\)
  3. \(\tfrac{x}{y}\) and \(y\)
  4. \(\tfrac{y}{x}\) and \(x\)
  5. \(\tfrac{y}{x}\) and \(y\)

Original exam question

Use equation (2)

\[\begin{aligned}h&=\frac{\Delta K_{\max}}{\Delta f}=\frac{0-(-y)}{x-0}=\frac{y}{x} \\ -W&=-y\quad\Rightarrow\quad W=y \\ W&=hf_0=\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)x=y\end{aligned}\]

Choice 5 follows from the slope and intercept. Equation (3) provides the check.

9. Nine Common Subjects 2022 · Question 23 (2565)

Electromagnetic waves at different frequencies are incident on the surface of a metal. The stopping potential is plotted against frequency as shown.

Let e be the electron charge and h the Planck constant in joule-seconds.

At frequency f, what is the maximum kinetic energy of the photoelectrons in electronvolts?

  1. \(\tfrac{hf}{e}-2\)
  2. \(\tfrac{hf}{e}+2\)
  3. \(\tfrac{hf}{e}+5\)
  4. \(hf-2e\)
  5. \(hf+2e\)

Original exam question

Use equation (2), (4)

\[\begin{aligned}eV_s&=hf-W\quad\Rightarrow\quad V_s=\frac{h}{e}f-\frac{W}{e} \\ -\frac{W}{e}&=-2\,\mathrm V\quad\Rightarrow\quad W=e(2\,\mathrm V)=2\,\mathrm{eV} \\ \frac{K_{\max}}{1\,\mathrm{eV}}&=\frac{hf}{1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J}-2\end{aligned}\]

Choice 1. The derivation uses e as the magnitude of the electron charge. With numerical SI values, dividing by the numerical value of e converts joules to electronvolts, giving hf/e − 2.

The −2 V intercept comes from extrapolating to f = 0. Vₛ in equation (4) is the stopping-potential magnitude in the emission regime. At cutoff on the simulation’s I–V graph, the signed voltage V equals −Vₛ.

10. Light intensity and particle counts

Light of intensity 0.30 W/m² is incident normally on 4.0 cm² for 2.0 s. Each photon carries 6.0 × 10⁻¹⁹ J. Assume every photon is absorbed and releases one electron.

a. Find the total incident light energy and the number of electrons emitted.

Use equation (7), (8)

\[\begin{aligned}E_{\mathrm{total}}&=I_{\mathrm{light}}A\Delta t=(0.30\,\mathrm{W/m^2})(4.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2})(2.0\,\mathrm s)=2.4\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm J \\ N&=\frac{E_{\mathrm{total}}}{E_\gamma}=\frac{2.4\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm J}{6.0\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J}=4.0\times10^{14}\end{aligned}\]

Use the area in square metres.

11. One light source, two metals

Light at the threshold frequency of metal X, whose work function is 4.00 eV, gives a stopping potential of 1.60 V when it shines on metal Y.

a. Find the photon energy and the work function of metal Y.

Use equation (3), (4), (2)

\[\begin{aligned}E_\gamma&=W_X=4.00\,\mathrm{eV} \\ K_{\max,Y}&=eV_s=1.60\,\mathrm{eV} \\ W_Y&=E_\gamma-K_{\max,Y}=(4.00-1.60)\,\mathrm{eV}=2.40\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

The same light delivers the same energy per photon to either metal.

Learning HubPage 1 / 13
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Energy of a single photon

In the photon model, an electron at the metal can absorb all the energy of one photon. The delivered energy depends on the light frequency.

SymbolMeaning
\(E_\gamma\)Energy per photon, in J or eV
\(f\)Light frequency, in Hz
\(\lambda\)Wavelength in vacuum, in m
\(h\)Planck constant · \(6.626\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s}\)
\(c\)Speed of light in vacuum · \(3.00\times10^8\,\mathrm{m/s}\)

Quantum theory gives energy proportional to frequency. In vacuum, \(c=f\lambda\), so frequency is light speed divided by wavelength.

\(E_\gamma=hf=\frac{hc}{\lambda}\)(1)

\(1\,\mathrm{eV}=1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\qquad 1\,\mathrm{nm}=10^{-9}\,\mathrm m\)

At fixed frequency, how does raising intensity change the energy per photon and the number of photons incident per second?

Energy per photon is unchanged by equation (1). More photons arrive each second over the same illuminated area.

Learning HubPage 2 / 13
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Work function and the emission threshold

The work function \(W\) is the minimum energy required for an electron to escape the metal surface. It depends on the metal and surface condition. The remaining energy is kinetic. Electrons requiring the least escape energy have the maximum kinetic energy \(K_{\max}\). With consistent units, energy conservation gives \(hf=W+K_{\max}\).

\(K_{\max}=hf-W\)(2)

Let \(f_0\) be the threshold frequency. At the threshold, the photon energy just matches the work function, and equation (2) gives zero maximum kinetic energy in the ideal limit.

\(W=hf_0\)(3)
Light frequencyEmission from the metal
\(f\lt f_0\)The photon energy is insufficient. No photoelectrons are emitted.
\(f=f_0\)Ideal threshold: the energy just suffices and maximum kinetic energy is zero. The lab displays a current once frequency exceeds the threshold.
\(f>f_0\)Emission is possible and maximum kinetic energy is positive.

In the table on the first page, why does the meter still read 0.00 µA at 6.00 × 10¹⁴ Hz when intensity rises from 50% to 80%?

More photons arrive, but by equation (1) each still carries the same energy, below the work function in equation (3).

Learning HubPage 3 / 13
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Metal thresholds and the energy graph

Mission 4 · Compare your class readings with the model table for three metals at 50% intensity and +1.00 V collector voltage.

Frequency (10¹⁴ Hz)A (µA)B (µA)C (µA)
6.402.000.000.00
7.102.002.000.00
7.602.002.002.00

Why can changing the metal at the same frequency reduce the current to zero?

At 6.40, A gives a current. At 7.10, B gives a current when compared with C. At 7.60, C gives a current. By equation (3), larger W requires larger f₀.

Compare equation (2) with a straight-line equation. Frequency is on the horizontal axis and maximum kinetic energy is on the vertical axis. All slopes equal h in consistent units. Each horizontal intercept is f₀. Extending a line to f = 0 gives a vertical intercept of −W.

Learning HubPage 4 / 13
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

From emission to the meter reading

The lab defines \(V=V_{\mathrm{collector}}-V_{\mathrm{emitter}}\). Positive V makes the collector attract electrons. With negative V, the electric field slows electrons moving toward the collector.

The electron has charge −e, where e is the elementary charge magnitude. Its electric potential energy changes by \(\Delta U=(-e)V\). When retarding voltage just brings the current to zero, let \(V_s\) be the stopping-potential magnitude. The signed axis reading is \(V=-V_s\). The fastest electrons need \(eV_s\) of energy to overcome the retarding field and reach the collection threshold.

\(K_{\max}=eV_s\)(4)

Use e = 1.602 × 10⁻¹⁹ C to answer in joules. For one electron, the numerical value of the energy in eV equals the numerical stopping-potential magnitude in volts.

The meter reads zero at 6.00 × 10¹⁴ Hz in Mission 2 and under teaching light at V = −0.50 V in Mission 3. What is different in the tube?

In Mission 2, the frequency is too low to emit electrons. In Mission 3, the light still causes emission, but the retarding voltage prevents electrons from reaching the collector.

Collector voltage relative to emitter V (V)I (µA)
+1.002.00

−0.251.00
−0.450.20
−0.500.00
−0.650.00

Which interval is marked by the last nonzero reading and the first zero? Distinguish signed V from the magnitude Vₛ.

At −0.45 V the current is 0.20 µA. At −0.50 V it is zero. The two samples bracket 0.45 < Vₛ ≤ 0.50 V. The first zero gives V ≈ −0.50 V or Vₛ ≈ 0.50 V, which equals the exact model cutoff for this preset.

Learning HubPage 6 / 13
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

From readings to a graph, speed and photon count

Vₛ is the stopping-potential magnitude and V is the collector voltage relative to the emitter. When sweeping toward negative voltage, estimate Vₛ as −V at the first zero-current reading.

Substitute the kinetic energy from equation (2) into (4), then divide by the electron charge magnitude e.

\(eV_s=hf-W\)

\(V_s=\frac{h}{e}f-\frac{W}{e}\)(5)

On the Vₛ versus f graph, the slope is h/e, the horizontal intercept is f₀, and the extrapolated vertical intercept is −W/e.

Let mₑ be the electron mass in kg and vₘₐₓ the maximum speed in m/s. Use the nonrelativistic kinetic energy in joules.

\(K_{\max}=\frac{1}{2}m_ev_{\max}^2\)(6)

Let I_light be irradiance in W/m², A the illuminated area in m², and Δt the time interval in seconds. For normally incident light with constant irradiance, intensity is total energy per area per time.

\(I_{\mathrm{light}}=\frac{E_{\mathrm{total}}}{A\Delta t}\)

\(E_{\mathrm{total}}=I_{\mathrm{light}}A\Delta t\)(7)

If every photon carries the same energy Eγ, let N be the incident photon count. The total energy is the sum of the energies of all N photons.

\(E_{\mathrm{total}}=NE_\gamma\)

\(N=\frac{E_{\mathrm{total}}}{E_\gamma}\)(8)
Learning HubPage 7 / 13
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Examples 1–2 · Calculations from photon energy

Calculation data · \(h=6.626\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s}\) · \(c=3.00\times10^8\,\mathrm{m/s}\) · \(1\,\mathrm{eV}=1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\) · \(1\,\mathrm{nm}=10^{-9}\,\mathrm m\)

Example 1 · Photon energy

Light in vacuum has a wavelength of 413.3 nm. Calculate the energy of one photon in joules and electronvolts.

Use equation (1)

\[\begin{aligned}\lambda&=413.3\times10^{-9}\,\mathrm m \\ E_\gamma&=\frac{hc}{\lambda}=\frac{(6.626\times10^{-34})(3.00\times10^8)}{413.3\times10^{-9}}=4.81\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J \\ E_\gamma&=\frac{4.81\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J}{1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm{J/eV}}\approx3.00\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Each photon carries about 3.00 eV.

Example 2 · Work function from threshold frequency

Metal A has a threshold frequency of about 6.04 × 10¹⁴ Hz. Find the work function in eV and the maximum kinetic energy under 3.00 eV photons.

Use equation (3), (2)

\[\begin{aligned}W&=hf_0=(6.626\times10^{-34})(6.04\times10^{14})=4.00\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\approx2.50\,\mathrm{eV} \\ K_{\max}&=E_\gamma-W=(3.00-2.50)\,\mathrm{eV}=0.50\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Photon energy exceeds W, so emission occurs.

Learning HubPage 8 / 13
PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Examples 3–4 · Check emission and compare metals

Calculation data · \(h=6.626\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s}\) · \(c=3.00\times10^8\,\mathrm{m/s}\) · \(1\,\mathrm{eV}=1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\) · \(1\,\mathrm{nm}=10^{-9}\,\mathrm m\)

Example 3 · A wavelength beyond threshold

Shine 600 nm light on metal A, whose work function is 2.50 eV. Decide whether electrons are emitted. If there is no emission, how much more energy per photon is needed to reach the threshold?

Use equation (1)

\[\begin{aligned}E_\gamma&=\frac{hc}{\lambda}=\frac{(6.626\times10^{-34})(3.00\times10^8)}{600\times10^{-9}}=3.31\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\approx2.07\,\mathrm{eV} \\ W-E_\gamma&\approx2.50-2.07=0.43\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

There is no emission. Each photon needs about 0.43 eV more to reach the threshold.

Example 4 · One light setting, three metals

Shine 3.00 eV photons on metals A, B and C, whose work functions are 2.50, 2.80 and 3.10 eV, respectively. Which metals emit electrons, and what is the maximum kinetic energy in eV for each emitting metal?

Use equation (2)

\[\begin{aligned}K_{\max,\mathrm A}&=E_\gamma-W_{\mathrm A}=(3.00-2.50)\,\mathrm{eV}=0.50\,\mathrm{eV} \\ K_{\max,\mathrm B}&=E_\gamma-W_{\mathrm B}=(3.00-2.80)\,\mathrm{eV}=0.20\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

A and B emit. Metal C has W greater than 3.00 eV, so it does not emit.

Examples 5–6 · Extract quantities from graphs

Example 5 · Planck constant from two graph points

For metal A, a frequency of 7.00 × 10¹⁴ Hz gives a maximum kinetic energy of 0.395 eV, and 9.00 × 10¹⁴ Hz gives 1.222 eV. Find the slope of the maximum kinetic energy versus frequency graph in eV·s, then use the first point to find the work function in eV.

Use equation (2)

\[\begin{aligned}h&=\frac{\Delta K_{\max}}{\Delta f}=\frac{1.222-0.395}{(9.00-7.00)\times10^{14}}=4.14\times10^{-15}\,\mathrm{eV\,s} \\ W&=hf-K_{\max}=(4.135\times10^{-15})(7.00\times10^{14})-0.395\approx2.50\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Retain the unrounded slope when calculating W.

Example 6 · Use the teaching I–V graph to find the work function

With 3.00 eV photons, the teaching graph shows 0.20 µA at a collector voltage of −0.45 V, and a first zero reading of 0.00 µA at −0.50 V. Estimate the stopping-potential magnitude in V, then find the maximum kinetic energy and work function in eV.

Use equation (4), (2)

\[\begin{aligned}V&\approx-0.50\,\mathrm V,\qquad V_s\approx0.50\,\mathrm V \\ K_{\max}&=eV_s\approx(1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm C)(0.50\,\mathrm V)=8.01\times10^{-20}\,\mathrm J=0.50\,\mathrm{eV} \\ W&=E_\gamma-K_{\max}\approx3.00-0.50=2.50\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

The measurements bracket 0.45 < Vₛ ≤ 0.50 V. The calculation uses the first zero as an estimate.

Example 7 · From your readings to a stopping-potential graph

Return to the lab, choose “Explore the tube”, and use the three frequency presets for metal A at 50% intensity. Record the last voltage with current and the first zero-current voltage, 0.01 V apart.

f (10¹⁴ Hz)Last V with I > 0 (V)First V with I = 0 (V)Vₛ (V)
6.50−0.18−0.190.19
7.25−0.49−0.500.50
8.00−0.80−0.810.81

Apply equation (5) to the Vₛ values in the table.

Plot the measured points, draw a trend line and extend it with dashes to read the intercepts. The measured points are above threshold.

Use equation (5)

\[\begin{aligned}\frac{h}{e}&\approx\frac{0.81-0.19}{(8.00-6.50)\times10^{14}}=4.13\times10^{-15}\,\mathrm{V\,s} \\ h&=e\frac{h}{e}\approx(1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm C)(4.13\times10^{-15}\,\mathrm{V\,s})\approx6.62\times10^{-34}\,\mathrm{J\,s} \\ f_0&\approx6.04\times10^{14}\,\mathrm{Hz} \\ -\frac{W}{e}&\approx-2.50\,\mathrm V\quad\Rightarrow\quad W\approx2.50\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

Changing the metal keeps the slope the same and shifts the intercepts according to W.

Examples 8–9 · Speed and particle counts

Example 8 · From stopping potential to speed

The lab gives a stopping potential of about 0.50 V for metal A. Find the maximum electron speed. The electron mass is 9.11 × 10⁻³¹ kg.

Use equation (4), (6)

\[\begin{aligned}K_{\max}&=eV_s=(1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm C)(0.50\,\mathrm V)=8.01\times10^{-20}\,\mathrm J \\ v_{\max}&=\sqrt{\frac{2K_{\max}}{m_e}}\approx\sqrt{\frac{2(8.01\times10^{-20}\,\mathrm J)}{9.11\times10^{-31}\,\mathrm{kg}}}\approx4.19\times10^5\,\mathrm{m/s}\end{aligned}\]

Use kinetic energy in joules to obtain speed in m/s.

Example 9 · From light intensity to photon count

Light of intensity 0.20 W/m² is incident normally on 2.0 cm² for 5.0 s. Each photon carries 4.0 × 10⁻¹⁹ J. Find the incident photon count. If every photon is absorbed and releases one electron, how many electrons are emitted?

Use equation (7), (8)

\[\begin{aligned}E_{\mathrm{total}}&=I_{\mathrm{light}} A\Delta t=(0.20\,\mathrm{W/m^2})(2.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2})(5.0\,\mathrm s)=2.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm J \\ N&=\frac{E_{\mathrm{total}}}{E_\gamma}=\frac{2.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm J}{4.0\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J}=5.0\times10^{14}\end{aligned}\]

The photon and electron counts are equal under the stated assumption.

PHYSICS · 19.2 · Photoelectric EffectRevision Notes · Read and revise

Example 10 · One light source, two metals

Example 10 · Use the first metal’s threshold to find the second metal’s work function

Shine light at the threshold frequency of sample metal X, whose work function is 3.60 eV, onto metal Y. The measured stopping potential is 0.90 V. Find the work function of Y.

Use equation (3), (4), (2)

\[\begin{aligned}E_\gamma&=hf_{0,X}=W_X=3.60\,\mathrm{eV} \\ K_{\max,Y}&=eV_s=0.90\,\mathrm{eV} \\ W_Y&=E_\gamma-K_{\max,Y}=(3.60-0.90)\,\mathrm{eV}=2.70\,\mathrm{eV}\end{aligned}\]

The light frequency stays the same when the metal changes.
